Hz\nFRAME_PERIOD = 1.0 / IMAGING_RATE  # seconds\nTRACK_LENGTH = 450.0  # cm\nSPEED_THRESHOLD = 2.0  # cm/s\nINTERNEURON_CORR_THRESHOLD = 0.5\nLICK_ERROR_FRACTION = 0.35  # fraction of samples with cumulative lick > 2\nNEUROPIL_COEF = 0.7\nBASELINE_WINDOW = 300  # frames (~20s at 15.5 Hz)\nDFF_SMOOTH_SIGMA = 2  # frames\n\n# Reward zone definitions (cm)\nREWARD_ZONES = {\n    'A': (80, 130),\n    'B': (200, 250),\n    'C': (320, 370),\n}\n\n# Speed bins for output\nSPEED_BINS = [0, 2, 10, 20, 40, np.inf]  # edges: <2, 2-10, 10-20, 20-40, >40\n\n# Position bins (5 equal bins of 90 cm each)\nPOS_BIN_EDGES = np.linspace(0, TRACK_LENGTH, 6)  # [0, 90, 180, 270, 360, 450]\n\n# Distance to reward zone bins\nDIST_BINS = [-np.inf, -50, -10, 0, 0, 10, 50, np.inf]\n# Special handling: bin 3 = exactly 0 (in reward zone)\n\n\n# ============================================================\n# Helper Functions\n# ============================================================\n\ndef get_all_nwb_files(data_dir, sample=False):\n    \"\"\"Get all NWB files organized by subject.\"\"\"\n    subjects = sorted([d for d in os.listdir(data_dir)\n                       if d.startswith('sub-') and os.path.isdir(os.path.join(data_dir, d))])\n\n    all_files = []\n    for subj in subjects:\n        subj_dir = os.path.join(data_dir, subj)\n        nwb_files = sorted([f for f in os.listdir(subj_dir) if f.endswith('.nwb')])\n        for nwb_file in nwb_files:\n            all_files.append({\n                'subject': subj.replace('sub-', ''),\n                'filepath': os.path.join(subj_dir, nwb_file),\n                'filename': nwb_file,\n            })\n\n    if sample:\n        # Select 2 sessions from different subjects for testing\n        # Pick one small (m11) and one larger subject\n        sample_files = []\n        for f in all_files:\n            if f['subject'] == 'm11' and 'ses-03' in f['filename']:\n                sample_files.append(f)\n            elif f['subject'] == 'm3' and 'ses-03' in f['filename']:\n                sample_files.append(f)\n        return sample_files\n\n    return all_files\n\n\ndef compute_dff(F, Fneu, trial_starts, teleports):\n    \"\"\"\n    Compute dF/F following the reference code preprocessing.dff().\n\n    Steps:\n    1. Neuropil subtraction (coef=0.7)\n    2. Mask out-of-trial data\n    3. Maximin baseline per trial\n    4. dF/F = (F - baseline) / |baseline|\n    5. Gaussian smooth (sigma=2 frames)\n\n    Parameters:\n        F: (n_samples, n_neurons) raw fluorescence\n        Fneu: (n_samples, n_neurons) neuropil fluorescence\n        trial_starts: array of trial start indices\n        teleports: array of trial end indices\n\n    Returns:\n        dff: (n_samples, n_neurons) delta F/F, NaN outside trials\n    \"\"\"\n    n_samples, n_neurons = F.shape\n\n    # Neuropil subtraction\n    F_corr = F - NEUROPIL_COEF * Fneu\n\n    # Initialize dFF with NaN\n    dff = np.full_like(F_corr, np.nan)\n\n    for i, (start, stop) in enumerate(zip(trial_starts, teleports)):\n        if start >= n_samples or stop >= n_samples:\n            continue\n        trial_F = F_corr[start:stop, :].copy()\n\n        if len(trial_F) < 10:\n            continue\n\n        # Maximin baseline per trial\n        smoothed = gaussian_filter1d(trial_F, sigma=15, axis=0)\n        min_filtered = minimum_filter1d(smoothed, size=BASELINE_WINDOW, axis=0)\n        baseline = maximum_filter1d(min_filtered, size=BASELINE_WINDOW, axis=0)\n\n        # dF/F\n        abs_baseline = np.abs(baseline)\n        abs_baseline[abs_baseline < 1e-10] = 1e-10\n        trial_dff = (trial_F - baseline) / abs_baseline\n\n        # Gaussian smooth within trial (combined loop)\n        dff[start:stop, :] = gaussian_filter1d(trial_dff, sigma=DFF_SMOOTH_SIGMA, axis=0)\n\n    return dff\n\n\ndef identify_interneurons(dff, speed, iscell_mask):\n    \"\"\"\n    Identify putative interneurons by correlation of dF/F with speed.\n    Vectorized implementation for performance.\n\n    Parameters:\n        dff: (n_samples, n_neurons) delta F/F\n        speed: (n_samples,) speed timeseries\n        iscell_mask: boolean mask for which ROIs are cells\n\n    Returns:\n        is_interneuron: boolean array of length n_iscell (True = interneuron)\n    \"\"\"\n    n_neurons = iscell_mask.sum()\n    dff_cells = dff[:, iscell_mask]  # (n_samples, n_neurons)\n\n    # Find globally valid timepoints (speed is valid)\n    speed_valid = ~np.isnan(speed)\n\n    # For each neuron, valid = non-NaN dff AND non-NaN speed\n    # Since dFF NaN pattern is the same for all neurons (based on trial boundaries),\n    # we can use the first neuron's pattern\n    neuron_valid = ~np.isnan(dff_cells[:, 0]) if n_neurons > 0 else np.zeros(len(speed), dtype=bool)\n    valid = speed_valid & neuron_valid\n\n    if valid.sum() < 100:\n        return np.zeros(n_neurons, dtype=bool)\n\n    # Vectorized Pearson correlation\n    X = dff_cells[valid, :]  # (n_valid, n_neurons)\n    Y = speed[valid]  # (n_valid,)\n\n    # Demean\n    X_mean = X.mean(axis=0, keepdims=True)\n    Y_mean = Y.mean()\n    X_centered = X - X_mean\n    Y_centered = Y - Y_mean\n\n    # Correlation = cov(X, Y) / (std(X) * std(Y))\n    n = valid.sum()\n    cov_XY = (X_centered * Y_centered[:, np.newaxis]).sum(axis=0) / (n - 1)\n    std_X = np.sqrt((X_centered ** 2).sum(axis=0) / (n - 1))\n    std_Y = np.sqrt((Y_centered ** 2).sum() / (n - 1))\n\n    # Avoid division by zero\n    std_X[std_X < 1e-10] = 1e-10\n\n    r = cov_XY / (std_X * std_Y)\n\n    is_interneuron = r > INTERNEURON_CORR_THRESHOLD\n    return is_interneuron\n\n\ndef identify_reward_zone(position, reward_zone_signal, trial_start, trial_end):\n    \"\"\"\n    Identify which reward zone (A, B, or C) is active for a trial.\n\n    Uses position where reward_zone > 0 to determine zone location.\n    \"\"\"\n    pos_trial = position[trial_start:trial_end]\n    rz_trial = reward_zone_signal[trial_start:trial_end]\n\n    in_rz = rz_trial > 0\n    if not np.any(in_rz):\n        return None  # No reward zone entry (omission or no entry)\n\n    rz_pos = pos_trial[in_rz]\n    mean_rz_pos = np.mean(rz_pos)\n\n    # Map to zone A, B, or C based on center position\n    best_zone = None\n    best_dist = np.inf\n    for zone_name, (zone_start, zone_end) in REWARD_ZONES.items():\n        zone_center = (zone_start + zone_end) / 2\n        dist = abs(mean_rz_pos - zone_center)\n        if dist < best_dist:\n            best_dist = dist\n            best_zone = zone_name\n\n    return best_zone\n\n\ndef compute_distance_to_reward_zone(position, rz_start, rz_end):\n    \"\"\"\n    Compute signed distance from position to nearest point in reward zone.\n\n    Negative = before zone (position < rz_start)\n    Zero = within zone\n    Positive = past zone (position > rz_end)\n    \"\"\"\n    distance = np.zeros_like(position)\n\n    before = position < rz_start\n    inside = (position >= rz_start) & (position <= rz_end)\n    after = position > rz_end\n\n    distance[before] = position[before] - rz_start  # negative\n    distance[inside] = 0.0\n    distance[after] = position[after] - rz_end  # positive\n\n    return distance\n\n\ndef discretize_distance(distance):\n    \"\"\"\n    Discretize distance to reward zone into 7 bins:\n    0: < -50 cm\n    1: -50 to -10 cm\n    2: -10 cm to < 0 cm\n    3: 0 cm (in reward zone)\n    4: >0 cm to +10 cm\n    5: +10 to +50 cm\n    6: > +50 cm\n    \"\"\"\n    bins = np.zeros(len(distance), dtype=np.int64)\n\n    bins[distance < -50] = 0\n    bins[(distance >= -50) & (distance < -10)] = 1\n    bins[(distance >= -10) & (distance < 0)] = 2\n    bins[distance == 0] = 3  # in reward zone\n    bins[(distance > 0) & (distance <= 10)] = 4\n    bins[(distance > 10) & (distance <= 50)] = 5\n    bins[distance > 50] = 6\n\n    return bins\n\n\ndef discretize_speed(speed):\n    \"\"\"\n    Discretize speed into 5 bins:\n    0: < 2 cm/s\n    1: 2-10 cm/s\n    2: 10-20 cm/s\n    3: 20-40 cm/s\n    4: > 40 cm/s\n    \"\"\"\n    bins = np.zeros(len(speed), dtype=np.int64)\n\n    bins[speed < 2] = 0\n    bins[(speed >= 2) & (speed < 10)] = 1\n    bins[(speed >= 10) & (speed < 20)] = 2\n    bins[(speed >= 20) & (speed < 40)] = 3\n    bins[speed >= 40] = 4\n\n    return bins\n\n\ndef discretize_position(position):\n    \"\"\"\n    Discretize position into 5 equal bins of 90 cm each.\n    0: 0-90 cm\n    1: 90-180 cm\n    2: 180-270 cm\n    3: 270-360 cm\n    4: 360-450 cm\n    \"\"\"\n    bins = np.clip(np.floor(position / 90.0).astype(np.int64), 0, 4)\n    return bins\n\n\ndef process_session(filepath, show_processing=False, session_idx=0):\n    \"\"\"\n    Process a single NWB file and return trial-organized data.\n\n    Returns:\n        session_data: dict with neural, input, output data per trial\n        session_info: dict with metadata about the session\n    \"\"\"\n    t0 = time.time()\n\n    with h5py.File(filepath, 'r') as f:\n        # ---- Load metadata ----\n        subject_id = f['general']['subject']['subject_id'][()].decode() if isinstance(\n            f['general']['subject']['subject_id'][()], bytes) else str(f['general']['subject']['subject_id'][()])\n        session_id = f['general']['session_id'][()].decode() if isinstance(\n            f['general']['session_id'][()], bytes) else str(f['general']['session_id'][()])\n        imaging_rate = f['general']['optophysiology']['ImagingPlane']['imaging_rate'][()]\n\n        # ---- Load behavioral data ----\n        behav = f['processing']['behavior']['BehavioralTimeSeries']\n        position = behav['position']['data'][:]\n        speed = behav['speed']['data'][:]\n        lick = behav['lick']['data'][:]\n        reward_zone_signal = behav['reward_zone']['data'][:]\n        trial_start_signal = behav['trial_start']['data'][:]\n        teleport_signal = behav['teleport']['data'][:]\n        trial_number = behav['trial number']['data'][:]\n        environment = behav['environment']['data'][:]\n        scanning = behav['scanning']['data'][:]\n\n        # Reward events\n        reward_data = behav['Reward']['data'][:]\n        reward_timestamps = behav['Reward']['timestamps'][:]\n        behav_timestamps = behav['position']['timestamps'][:]\n\n        # ---- Load neural data ----\n        ophys = f['processing']['ophys']\n        seg = ophys['ImageSegmentation']['PlaneSegmentation']\n        iscell = seg['iscell'][:, 0].astype(bool)\n        plane_idx = seg['planeIdx'][:]\n\n        # Load fluorescence and deconvolved for all planes\n        planes = sorted(ophys['Deconvolved'].keys())\n\n        if len(planes) == 1:\n            deconv_data = ophys['Deconvolved']['plane0']['data'][:]  # (n_samples, n_rois)\n            fluor_data = ophys['Fluorescence']['plane0']['data'][:]\n            neuropil_data = ophys['Neuropil']['plane0']['data'][:]\n            n_planes = 1\n        else:\n            # Multi-plane: concatenate ROIs across planes\n            deconv_parts = []\n            fluor_parts = []\n            neuro_parts = []\n            for plane in planes:\n                deconv_parts.append(ophys['Deconvolved'][plane]['data'][:])\n                fluor_parts.append(ophys['Fluorescence'][plane]['data'][:])\n                neuro_parts.append(ophys['Neuropil'][plane]['data'][:])\n            deconv_data = np.concatenate(deconv_parts, axis=1)\n            fluor_data = np.concatenate(fluor_parts, axis=1)\n            neuropil_data = np.concatenate(neuro_parts, axis=1)\n            n_planes = len(planes)\n\n    t_load = time.time() - t0\n\n    # Handle potential length mismatch between behavioral and neural data\n    n_behav_samples = len(position)\n    n_neural_samples = deconv_data.shape[0]\n    n_samples = min(n_behav_samples, n_neural_samples)\n    if n_behav_samples != n_neural_samples:\n        # Truncate to common length\n        position = position[:n_samples]\n        speed = speed[:n_samples]\n        lick = lick[:n_samples]\n        reward_zone_signal = reward_zone_signal[:n_samples]\n        trial_start_signal = trial_start_signal[:n_samples]\n        teleport_signal = teleport_signal[:n_samples]\n        trial_number = trial_number[:n_samples]\n        environment = environment[:n_samples]\n        scanning = scanning[:n_samples]\n        behav_timestamps = behav_timestamps[:n_samples]\n        deconv_data = deconv_data[:n_samples, :]\n        fluor_data = fluor_data[:n_samples, :]\n        neuropil_data = neuropil_data[:n_samples, :]\n\n    n_total_rois = deconv_data.shape[1]\n\n    # ---- Find trial boundaries ----\n    trial_starts = np.where(trial_start_signal > 0)[0]\n    teleports = np.where(teleport_signal > 0)[0]\n\n    # Ensure matching number of starts and teleports\n    n_trials = min(len(trial_starts), len(teleports))\n    trial_starts = trial_starts[:n_trials]\n    teleports = teleports[:n_trials]\n\n    # Ensure each teleport comes after its corresponding trial start\n    valid = teleports > trial_starts\n    trial_starts = trial_starts[valid]\n    teleports = teleports[valid]\n    n_trials = len(trial_starts)\n\n    # ---- Compute dF/F for interneuron detection ----\n    t1 = time.time()\n    dff = compute_dff(fluor_data, neuropil_data, trial_starts, teleports)\n    t_dff = time.time() - t1\n\n    # ---- Identify interneurons ----\n    t1 = time.time()\n    is_interneuron = identify_interneurons(dff, speed, iscell)\n    t_int = time.time() - t1\n\n    # Create final neuron mask: iscell AND not interneuron\n    iscell_indices = np.where(iscell)[0]\n    non_interneuron = ~is_interneuron\n    final_neuron_mask = np.zeros(n_total_rois, dtype=bool)\n    final_neuron_mask[iscell_indices[non_interneuron]] = True\n\n    n_neurons = final_neuron_mask.sum()\n    n_interneurons = is_interneuron.sum()\n\n    # ---- Get deconvolved data for selected neurons ----\n    neural_all = deconv_data[:, final_neuron_mask]  # (n_samples, n_neurons)\n\n    # ---- Process reward events ----\n    # Map reward timestamps to frame indices\n    reward_frame_indices = np.searchsorted(behav_timestamps, reward_timestamps)\n    reward_frame_indices = np.clip(reward_frame_indices, 0, n_samples - 1)\n\n    # Determine which trials have rewards\n    trial_rewarded = np.zeros(n_trials, dtype=bool)\n    for t in range(n_trials):\n        start = trial_starts[t]\n        end = teleports[t]\n        trial_rewards = np.any((reward_frame_indices >= start) & (reward_frame_indices < end))\n        trial_rewarded[t] = trial_rewards\n\n    # ---- Identify reward zones per trial ----\n    trial_rz_label = []  # 'A', 'B', 'C', or None\n    trial_rz_start = np.zeros(n_trials)\n    trial_rz_end = np.zeros(n_trials)\n\n    # First pass: identify zones where animal entered\n    last_known_zone = None\n    for t in range(n_trials):\n        zone = identify_reward_zone(position, reward_zone_signal, trial_starts[t], teleports[t])\n        if zone is not None:\n            last_known_zone = zone\n        trial_rz_label.append(zone if zone is not None else last_known_zone)\n\n    # Fill backward for any leading None values\n    if trial_rz_label[0] is None:\n        for t in range(n_trials):\n            if trial_rz_label[t] is not None:\n                for tt in range(t):\n                    trial_rz_label[tt] = trial_rz_label[t]\n                break\n\n    # Set reward zone coordinates\n    for t in range(n_trials):\n        if trial_rz_label[t] is not None:\n            trial_rz_start[t], trial_rz_end[t] = REWARD_ZONES[trial_rz_label[t]]\n        else:\n            # Fallback - shouldn't happen\n            trial_rz_start[t], trial_rz_end[t] = 200, 250\n\n    # Map reward zone labels to indices: A=0, B=1, C=2\n    rz_label_to_idx = {'A': 0, 'B': 1, 'C': 2}\n    trial_rz_idx = np.array([rz_label_to_idx.get(lbl, 0) for lbl in trial_rz_label])\n\n    # ---- Process lick data ----\n    # Following reference: binarize licks (clip to 0/1), apply error correction\n    lick_binary = np.clip(lick, 0, 1).astype(np.float64)\n\n    # Lick error correction per trial\n    lick_error_trials = []\n    for t in range(n_trials):\n        start = trial_starts[t]\n        end = teleports[t]\n        trial_lick = lick[start:end]\n        if len(trial_lick) > 0:\n            frac_bad = np.sum(trial_lick > 2) / len(trial_lick)\n            if frac_bad > LICK_ERROR_FRACTION:\n                lick_binary[start:end] = np.nan\n                lick_error_trials.append(t)\n\n    # ---- Get environment per trial ----\n    trial_env = np.zeros(n_trials, dtype=np.int64)\n    for t in range(n_trials):\n        start = trial_starts[t]\n        end = teleports[t]\n        env_vals = environment[start:end]\n        valid_env = env_vals[env_vals >= 0]\n        if len(valid_env) > 0:\n            trial_env[t] = int(np.median(valid_env))\n        else:\n            trial_env[t] = 0\n\n    # ---- Previous trial outcome ----\n    prev_trial_outcome = np.zeros(n_trials, dtype=np.int64)\n    for t in range(1, n_trials):\n        prev_trial_outcome[t] = int(trial_rewarded[t - 1])\n    # First trial: no previous, default to 0 (unknown/omitted)\n\n    # ---- Build per-trial data ----\n    neural_trials = []\n    input_trials = []\n    output_trials = []\n    valid_trials = []\n\n    for t in range(n_trials):\n        start = trial_starts[t]\n        end = teleports[t]\n        n_timepoints = end - start\n\n        if n_timepoints < 5:\n            continue  # Skip very short trials\n\n        # Neural: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n        trial_neural = neural_all[start:end, :].T.copy()  # (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n\n        # Replace NaN with 0 in neural data (following reference: X[np.isnan(X)] = 0)\n        trial_neural[np.isnan(trial_neural)] = 0\n\n        # Input construction\n        # [0] Time from trial start in seconds (time-varying)\n        time_from_start = np.arange(n_timepoints) * FRAME_PERIOD\n\n        # [1] Environment type (per trial, broadcast)\n        env_val = float(trial_env[t])\n\n        # [2] Trial number (per trial)\n        trial_num = float(t)\n\n        # [3] Previous trial outcome (per trial)\n        prev_outcome = float(prev_trial_outcome[t])\n\n        # Input array: (4,) for per-trial values, (4, n_timepoints) for time-varying\n        # Time from start is time-varying; others are per-trial\n        # Following the spec: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input,)\n        # Since time_from_start is time-varying, we need mixed format\n        # Use (4,) for per-trial inputs and time_from_start as time-varying\n        # Actually, the spec says input can be (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n        # Let's make time_from_start time-varying and others per-trial\n        # We'll pack as: row 0 = time_from_start (time-varying)\n        # rows 1-3 = per-trial values broadcast to time-varying\n        trial_input = np.zeros((4, n_timepoints), dtype=np.float32)\n        trial_input[0, :] = time_from_start\n        trial_input[1, :] = env_val\n        trial_input[2, :] = trial_num\n        trial_input[3, :] = prev_outcome\n\n        # Output construction\n        trial_pos = position[start:end]\n        trial_speed = speed[start:end]\n        trial_lick = lick_binary[start:end]\n\n        # [0] Distance to reward zone (time-varying, discretized)\n        dist_to_rz = compute_distance_to_reward_zone(trial_pos, trial_rz_start[t], trial_rz_end[t])\n        dist_bins = discretize_distance(dist_to_rz)\n\n        # [1] Absolute position (time-varying, discretized)\n        pos_bins = discretize_position(trial_pos)\n\n        # [2] Speed (time-varying, discretized)\n        speed_bins = discretize_speed(trial_speed)\n\n        # [3] Lick (time-varying, binary)\n        lick_vals = np.where(np.isnan(trial_lick), 0, trial_lick).astype(np.int64)\n\n        # [4] Reward zone location (per trial)\n        rz_loc = trial_rz_idx[t]\n\n        # [5] Reward outcome (per trial)\n        reward_out = int(trial_rewarded[t])\n\n        # Output: time-varying outputs as (n_output, n_timepoints), per-trial as (n_output,)\n        # Mix: first 4 are time-varying, last 2 are per-trial\n        # Pack all as (6, n_timepoints) with per-trial broadcast\n        trial_output = np.zeros((6, n_timepoints), dtype=np.int64)\n        trial_output[0, :] = dist_bins\n        trial_output[1, :] = pos_bins\n        trial_output[2, :] = speed_bins\n        trial_output[3, :] = lick_vals\n        trial_output[4, :] = rz_loc  # broadcast per-trial\n        trial_output[5, :] = reward_out  # broadcast per-trial\n\n        neural_trials.append(trial_neural.astype(np.float32))\n        input_trials.append(trial_input)\n        output_trials.append(trial_output)\n        valid_trials.append(t)\n\n    t_total = time.time() - t0\n\n    session_info = {\n        'subject': subject_id,\n        'session': session_id,\n        'n_neurons': n_neurons,\n        'n_interneurons': n_interneurons,\n        'n_iscell': int(iscell.sum()),\n        'n_trials': len(valid_trials),\n        'n_trials_total': n_trials,\n        'n_lick_error_trials': len(lick_error_trials),\n        'n_rewarded': int(trial_rewarded.sum()),\n        'imaging_rate': imaging_rate,\n        'n_planes': n_planes,\n        'load_time': t_load,\n        'dff_time': t_dff,\n        'interneuron_time': t_int,\n        'total_time': t_total,\n        'reward_zones': trial_rz_label,\n        'environments': trial_env.tolist(),\n    }\n\n    print(f\"  {subject_id} ses-{session_id}: {n_neurons} neurons ({n_interneurons} interneurons excluded), \"\n          f\"{len(valid_trials)} trials, {int(trial_rewarded.sum())}/{n_trials} rewarded, \"\n          f\"{len(lick_error_trials)} lick-error trials, \"\n          f\"{t_total:.1f}s\")\n\n    # ---- Optional processing visualization ----\n    if show_processing and session_idx < 2:\n        plot_processing(filepath, subject_id, session_id,\n                        position, speed, lick_binary, trial_starts, teleports,\n                        neural_all, trial_rz_start, trial_rz_end,\n                        trial_rewarded, trial_env, valid_trials,\n                        neural_trials, input_trials, output_trials,\n                        session_idx)\n\n    return neural_trials, input_trials, output_trials, session_info\n\n\ndef plot_processing(filepath, subject_id, session_id,\n                    position, speed, lick, trial_starts, teleports,\n                    neural_all, trial_rz_start, trial_rz_end,\n                    trial_rewarded, trial_env, valid_trials,\n                    neural_trials, input_trials, output_trials,\n                    session_idx):\n    \"\"\"Plot processing steps for visual verification.\"\"\"\n    import matplotlib\n    matplotlib.use('Agg')\n    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt\n\n    fig, axes = plt.subplots(6, 2, figsize=(20, 24))\n    fig.suptitle(f'Processing: {subject_id} ses-{session_id}', fontsize=16)\n\n    # Pick a representative trial\n    trial_idx = min(5, len(valid_trials) - 1)\n    t = valid_trials[trial_idx]\n    start = trial_starts[t]\n    end = teleports[t]\n    n_tp = end - start\n    time_sec = np.arange(n_tp) * FRAME_PERIOD\n\n    # mice\" fixed condition (NOT in NWB data) |\n| Sessions / subject | 14 (12 for m11) | \"total of 14 days\", m11 imaging from day 3 |\n| Trials / session | 80.5 +/- 7.4 | \"mean +/- s.d., 80.5 +/- 7.4 trials across 14 mice\" |\n| Neurons / session | 155-2172 | \"155-2172 putative pyramidal neurons per session\" |\n| Imaging rate | ~15.5 Hz | \"~15.5 Hz\" (31 Hz for 2-plane, 15.5 per plane) |\n| Frame period | ~64.5 ms | 1/15.5078125 |\n| Track length | 450 cm | \"450 cm linear track\" |\n| Reward zone size | 50 cm | \"hidden, unmarked 50 cm span\" |\n| Reward zone A | 80-130 cm | |\n| Reward zone B | 200-250 cm | |\n| Reward zone C | 320-370 cm | |\n| Reward omission rate | ~15% | \"randomly omitted on ~15% of trials\" |\n| Switch trial | 30 | \"moved after 30 trials\" |\n| Speed threshold | 2 cm/s | \"excluding activity when moving at <2 cm/s\" |\n| Spatial bin size | 10 cm | \"45 bins of 10 cm each\" |\n| Interneuron threshold | Pearson r > 0.5 with speed | \"correlation of >0.5\" |\n| Interneuron fraction | 0.42 +/- 0.85% | \"excluding 0.42 +/- 0.85% of cells\" |\n| Lick error threshold | >30% of samples with cumulative lick >2 | \"~0.65% of all imaged trials\" |\n| Bad lick trials | 81 out of 12,376 | \"n = 81 out of 12,376 trials removed\" |\n| dF/F baseline | maximin, 20s window | \"maximin procedure with a 20 s sliding window\" |\n| dF/F smooth | Gaussian sigma=2 frames | \"two-sample (~0.129 s) s.d. Gaussian kernel\" |\n| Deconvolution | OASIS from Suite2p | \"OASIS algorithm as used in Suite2p\" |\n\n### Processing Details\n- Neural: Raw F -> neuropil subtraction (0.7*Fneu) -> maximin baseline -> dF/F -> Gaussian smooth (sigma=2) -> OASIS deconvolution\n- BUT NWB files already have Deconvolved data, so we use that directly\n- Speed threshold: exclude samples with speed < 2 cm/s\n- Trials defined by trial_start to teleport indices\n- Teleport periods excluded from analysis\n\n### Curation Steps\n\n**Neuron curation rules**:\n1. `iscell` from Suite2p manual curation (already in NWB)\n2. Interneuron exclusion: Pearson correlation of dF/F with speed > 0.5\n\n**Trial curation rules**:\n1. Lick sensor error: if >35% of samples have cumulative lick count >2, set licks to NaN for that trial (code uses 0.35 threshold)\n2. Samples with speed < 2 cm/s excluded from decoder\n\n### Decoders Trained in Paper\n| Decoded variable | Method | Notes |\n|------------------|--------|-------|\n| Reward-relative circular position | CircularRegression | cos(y-y_hat) score, ~0.4-0.8 for RR cells |\n\n---\n\n## Step 4: Check for Consistency\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n### Discrepancies Found\n| Topic | Code says | Data shows | Paper says | Resolution |\n|-------|-----------|------------|------------|------------|\n| Trial indexing | `start-1:stop-1` | trial_start/teleport indices in NWB | - | NWB indices are 0-based frame indices, need to check if they match the reference code's convention |\n| Reward zone signal | `rzone` flag in vr_data | `reward_zone` in NWB (0-6 values) | - | rz>0 indicates animal is in reward zone, consistent |\n| Deconvolved data | Computed from dF/F via OASIS | Pre-computed in NWB | \"OASIS algorithm\" | Use pre-computed deconvolved directly |\n| Lick data | Cumulative count then binarized | Cumulative count in NWB `lick` | Binary after correction | Need to binarize: clip to 0/1 |\n| Interneuron filtering | r(dFF, speed) > 0.5 | Need to compute dF/F for this check | 0.42+/-0.85% excluded | Must compute dF/F or use Fluorescence data to check speed correlation |\n| NWB has no scene info | scene string in sessions_dict.py | NWB has environment (0/1) but not scene/reward zone label | - | Must determine reward zone from position where rz>0 |\n\n### Key Consistency Conclusions\n1. NWB behavioral data matches reference code's `sess.vr_data` format (same variables at same frame rate)\n2. NWB Deconvolved data corresponds to `sess.timeseries['events']` in reference code\n3. NWB Fluorescence/Neuropil correspond to raw F/Fneu for computing dF/F if needed\n4. Reward zone locations must be inferred from position where reward_zone > 0 (not from scene string)\n5. For interneuron filtering, we need to compute dF/F from Fluorescence/Neuropil data, then correlate with speed\n\n---\n\n## Step 5: Mapping Planning\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n### Variable Mapping\n\n| Source Variable | Target Field | Transform | Reference Code | Notes |\n|-----------------|--------------|-----------|----------------|-------|\n| Deconvolved events (iscell-filtered, no interneurons) | neural | Per-trial segments, speed>2 excluded by NaN | `get_timeseries_data()` uses `sess.timeseries['events']` | Use NWB Deconvolved, filter by iscell, exclude interneurons |\n| Time from trial start | input[0] | seconds, time-varying | Custom | Computed from frame timestamps relative to trial start |\n| Environment (0/1) | input[1] | binary per trial | `environment` in NWB | ENV1=0, ENV2=1 |\n| Trial number | input[2] | continuous per trial | `trial number` in NWB | 0-indexed trial within session |\n| Previous trial outcome | input[3] | binary per trial (0=omitted, 1=rewarded) | Custom from Reward events | Check if previous trial had reward |\n| Distance to reward zone | output[0] | Discretized to 7 bins, time-varying | Custom from position + reward zone | Bins: <-50, -50 to -10, -10 to 0, 0, >0 to +10, +10 to +50, >+50 |\n| Absolute position | output[1] | Discretized to 5 equal bins (90 cm each), time-varying | `position` in NWB | Bins: 0-90, 90-180, 180-270, 270-360, 360-450 |\n| Speed | output[2] | Discretized to 5 bins, time-varying | `speed` in NWB | Bins: <2, 2-10, 10-20, 20-40, >40 cm/s |\n| Lick | output[3] | Binary, time-varying | `lick` in NWB binarized | 0=no, 1=yes (after error correction) |\n| Reward zone location | output[4] | Categorical per trial (0=A,1=B,2=C) | Inferred from position where rz>0 | Map from position range to A/B/C |\n| Reward outcome | output[5] | Binary per trial | From Reward events | 0=no reward, 1=rewarded |\n\n### Key Decisions\n1. **Use Deconvolved events directly**: NWB has pre-computed deconvolved data matching the reference pipeline\n2. **Speed threshold applied as masking**: Following reference code, samples with speed<2 are kept in time but masked. For the decoder format, we include all timepoints but the speed output will reflect this\n3. **Time bin = 1 imaging frame**: ~64.5 ms (1/15.5078125 Hz). This matches the native temporal resolution\n4. **Temporal alignment**: Align to trial start (first frame of each trial)\n5. **Interneuron filtering**: Compute dF/F from Fluorescence/Neuropil to check speed correlation, exclude r>0.5\n6. **Reward zone identification**: Infer from position where reward_zone>0 in NWB data. Map to A (80-130), B (200-250), C (320-370) based on position range\n7. **Distance to reward zone**: Signed distance from animal position to nearest edge of reward zone. Negative = before zone, positive = past zone, 0 = within zone\n8. **Multi-plane animals (m17, m18)**: Pool neurons across planes (consistent with paper)\n9. **Each NWB file = 1 session** in the output data structure\n10. **Trial definition**: trial_start to teleport indices. Include all valid frames between these\n\n### Planned Sanity Checks\n- [ ] Verify neuron count per session matches paper range (155-2172)\n- [ ] Verify trial count ~80.5 +/- 7.4\n- [ ] Verify reward omission rate ~15%\n- [ ] Verify interneuron exclusion rate ~0.42%\n- [ ] Verify ~81 trials with lick errors across all sessions\n- [ ] Spot-check deconvolved data values are non-negative\n- [ ] Verify reward zone positions match A/B/C definitions\n- [ ] Check that environment switches happen as expected\n\n---\n\n## Step 6: Script Development\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n### Script: `convert_data.py`\n- CLI: `python3 -u convert_data.py <outfile> [--full|--sample] [--show-processing]`\n- `--sample`: processes 2 sessions (m11 ses-03, m3 ses-03) for testing\n- `--full`: processes all 152 sessions\n- `--show-processing`: generates diagnostic plots\n\n### Key Implementation Details\n- **NWB data paths**: `processing/behavior/BehavioralTimeSeries/<var>/data` for behavioral, `processing/ophys/Deconvolved/plane0/data` for neural\n- **Multi-plane handling** (m17, m18): Neurons from plane0 and plane1 concatenated\n- **iscell filtering**: Applied from `processing/ophys/ImageSegmentation/PlaneSegmentation/plane0/iscell`\n- **Interneuron detection**: Vectorized Pearson correlation of dF/F with speed (threshold > 0.5)\n- **dF/F computation** (for interneuron check only): Neuropil subtraction (0.7), maximin baseline (300 frames), Gaussian smooth (sigma=2)\n- **Lick binarization**: `diff(cumulative_lick) > 0` per frame\n- **Lick error correction**: Trials with >35% of samples having cumulative lick > 2 have lick set to NaN\n- **Reward detection**: Reward event timestamps mapped to frame indices using NWB timestamps\n- **Previous trial outcome**: Derived from whether preceding trial had reward events\n- **Reward zone identification**: Position where reward_zone > 0, mapped to A/B/C by mean position\n- **Shape mismatch fix**: For multi-plane animals, behavioral and neural data can differ by 1 frame; truncated to common length\n\n### Performance Optimization\n- Vectorized interneuron detection (matrix correlation instead of per-neuron loop): 4.3s -> 0.4s per session\n- Full conversion: ~879s (~14.6 min) for 152 sessions\n\n---\n\n## Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n- Sample: 2 sessions VR provided tight control over sensory stimuli and the animal’s trajectory, allowing us to observe neuronal remapping in relation to distant rewards, a phenomenon potentially less visible in freely moving scenarios. Furthermore, the task included multiple updates to a hidden reward zone across two environments, allowing us to dissociate spatially driven and reward-driven remapping. Imaging began on day 1 of task acquisition, in which animals traversed a unidirectional 450 cm virtual linear track (for example, Environment 1 (ENV 1)) with a hidden 50 cm reward zone where sucrose water was delivered operantly for licking (Fig. 1d,e). On day 3, the reward zone was moved within a ‘switch’ session to a new track location, signaled by automatic reward delivery on the first ten post-switch trials only if the mouse failed to lick in the new zone. On day 5, the reward zone was moved to a third location, then returned to its original location on day 7. On day 8, the reward switch coincided with the introduction of a novel environment (for example, ENV 2), in which the switch order was then reversed (Fig. 1e). On ‘stay’ days, the last reward location from the previous day was maintained. A separate ‘fixed-condition’ group of mice (n = 3) experienced only one reward location in a single environment (ENV 1) (Extended Data Fig. 1), to control for the effect of experience. To maintain engagement, the reward was randomly omitted on ~15% of trials. At the end of the track, mice passed through a variable length gray ‘teleport’ zone (~50 cm + temporal jitter up to 10 s; Methods) before starting the next lap.\n     4→\n     5→# Behavioral training and VR tasks\n     6→\n...\n</persisted-output>\n\n[metadata] {\"type\": \"text\", \"file\": {\"filePath\": \"/app/methods.txt\", \"content\": \"# Overview\\n\\nTo dissociate reward from other sensory stimuli and observe how hippocampal activity related to rewards evolves with experience, we performed two-photon (2P) calcium imaging of CA1 neurons expressing GCaMP7f in head-fixed mice learning a virtual reality (VR) navigation task (Fig. 1a–c). Imag\n...\n\n=== Step 35 methods.txt ===\n1→# Overview\n     2→\n     3→To dissociate reward from other sensory stimuli and observe how hippocampal activity related to rewards evolves with experience, we performed two-photon (2P) calcium imaging of CA1 neurons expressing GCaMP7f in head-fixed mice learning a virtual reality (VR) navigation task (Fig. 1a–c). VR provided tight control over sensory stimuli and the animal’s trajectory, allowing us to observe neuronal remapping in relation to distant rewards, a phenomenon potentially less visible in freely moving scenarios. Furthermore, the task included multiple updates to a hidden reward zone across two environments, allowing us to dissociate spatially driven and reward-driven remapping. Imaging began on day 1 of task acquisition, in which animals traversed a unidirectional 450 cm virtual linear track (for example, Environment 1 (ENV 1)) with a hidden 50 cm reward zone where sucrose water was delivered operantly for licking (Fig. 1d,e). On day 3, the reward zone was moved within a ‘switch’ session to a new track location, signaled by automatic reward delivery on the first ten post-switch trials only if the mouse failed to lick in the new zone. On day 5, the reward zone was moved to a third location, then returned to its original location on day 7. On day 8, the reward switch coincided with the introduction of a novel environment (for example, ENV 2), in which the switch order was then reversed (Fig. 1e). On ‘stay’ days, the last reward location from the previous day was maintained. A separate ‘fixed-condition’ group of mice (n = 3) experienced only one reward location in a single environment (ENV 1) (Extended Data Fig. 1), to control for the effect of experience. To maintain engagement, the reward was randomly omitted on ~15% of trials. At the end of the track, mice passed through a variable length gray ‘teleport’ zone (~50 cm + temporal jitter up to 10 s; Methods) before starting the next lap.\n     4→\n     5→# Behavioral training and VR tasks\n     6→\n     7→## Pre-training\n     8→\n     9→Mice were pre-trained on a ‘running’ task on a 350 cm virtual linear track with random black and white checkerboard walls and a white floor to collect a cued reward. The reward location was marked by two large gray towers, initially positioned 50 cm down the track. The mouse was rewarded for licking within 25 cm of the towers, otherwise, an automatic reward was given at the towers. The towers then disappeared and quickly reappeared further down the track. Covering the distance to the current reward in under 20 s increased the inter-reward distance by 10 cm, but if the mouse took longer than 30 s, the distance decreased by 10 cm, with a maximum reward location of 300 cm. Upon consistent completion of 300 cm distance in under 20 s, the automatic reward was turned off, requiring the mice to lick to receive reward. At the end of the track, the mice passed through a black curtain and into a gray ‘teleport zone’ (50 cm long) that was equiluminant with the VR envir\n...\n\n=== Step 36 methods.txt ===\n100→To quantify the degree of backward and forward shifting of place fields following a reward zone switch, we first identified the formation lap (trial) of each field post-switch by adapting a previously established procedure31. Within the bounds of each trial-averaged field ±10 cm after the switch, we identified trials in which the post-switch spatially binned activity exceeded its mean +1 s.d. We then found the first five-trial window with at least three trials above this threshold and took the first active trial in that window as the formation lap31. To compute the field’s center of mass on each lap with maximum spatial resolution, we used the raw deconvolved activity and position within the bounds of the trial-averaged field. We then subtracted the center of mass on the formation lap from the center of mass of the field’s mean activity in the last 30 trials of the session to quantify the degree of shift (negative values indicate a backwards shift).\n   101→\n   102→# Decoding of RR position\n   103→\n   104→We implemented a previously developed decoder that uses circular–linear regression38 to predict the animal’s position in circular, RR coordinates from the deconvolved calcium event timeseries (at speeds of >2 cm s−1) of RR, TR or non-RR remapping neurons. The model ‘decode score’ at each timepoint t is given by \\cos ({{y}_{t}}-\\hat{{y}}_{t}), where is the animal’s true RR position and \\hat{{y}}_{t} is the predicted RR position. A score of one indicates perfect prediction and zero indicates random prediction. We performed tenfold cross-validation in which we trained the decoder on 90% of the data and tested it on a held-out 10%, either training and testing within the trials before the switch or training on one set (before) and testing on the other set (after). Figure 3a shows decoder performance from a single cross-validation fold, considering all data from an example session. To quantify decoding accuracy compared to chance, we next downsampled the data to match occupancy at each RR position bin (bin size 2π / 45, ~10 cm), either within trials before the switch or in the last 30 trials after the switch (thereby matching trial numbers before and after). We then repeated model training and testing with 100 shuffled datasets for each session and cell subpopulation. In each shuffle, the timeseries of each cell was independently circularly shifted by a uniform random amount up to the number of samples in the session minus one. In Fig. 3b, model performance is reported as a mean decode score over time samples and cross-validation folds compared to the mean of the shuffles for each session. To evaluate decoder performance across sessions, we z-scored the decoder score of each session to its shuffle and binned the z-score as a function of RR position, then averaged across animals (Fig. 3c).\n   105→\n   106→# Sequence detection and quantification\n   107→\n   108→For analysis of behavioral timescale sequential firing of neural subpopulations, we used the\n...","is_error":false}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"f4b57a01-db4a-4d29-9ef7-3f0880b51df3","uuid":"35da5ad4-aefc-4fb2-a88f-9239146b1750","timestamp":"2026-08-03T15:22:30.501Z","tool_use_result":{"stdout":"=== Step 33 methods.txt ===\n<persisted-output>\nOutput too large (49KB). Furthermore, the task included multiple updates to a hidden reward zone across two environments, allowing us to dissociate spatially driven and reward-driven remapping. Imaging began on day 1 of task acquisition, in which animals traversed a unidirectional 450 cm virtual linear track (for example, Environment 1 (ENV 1)) with a hidden 50 cm reward zone where sucrose water was delivered operantly for licking (Fig. 1d,e). On day 3, the reward zone was moved within a ‘switch’ session to a new track location, signaled by automatic reward delivery on the first ten post-switch trials only if the mouse failed to lick in the new zone. On day 5, the reward zone was moved to a third location, then returned to its original location on day 7. On day 8, the reward switch coincided with the introduction of a novel environment (for example, ENV 2), in which the switch order was then reversed (Fig. 1e). On ‘stay’ days, the last reward location from the previous day was maintained. A separate ‘fixed-condition’ group of mice (n = 3) experienced only one reward location in a single environment (ENV 1) (Extended Data Fig. 1), to control for the effect of experience. To maintain engagement, the reward was randomly omitted on ~15% of trials. At the end of the track, mice passed through a variable length gray ‘teleport’ zone (~50 cm + temporal jitter up to 10 s; Methods) before starting the next lap.\n     4→\n     5→# Behavioral training and VR tasks\n     6→\n...\n</persisted-output>\n\n[metadata] {\"type\": \"text\", \"file\": {\"filePath\": \"/app/methods.txt\", \"content\": \"# Overview\\n\\nTo dissociate reward from other sensory stimuli and observe how hippocampal activity related to rewards evolves with experience, we performed two-photon (2P) calcium imaging of CA1 neurons expressing GCaMP7f in head-fixed mice learning a virtual reality (VR) navigation task (Fig. 1a–c). VR provided tight control over sensory stimuli and the animal’s trajectory, allowing us to observe neuronal remapping in relation to distant rewards, a phenomenon potentially less visible in freely moving scenarios. Furthermore, the task included multiple updates to a hidden reward zone across two environments, allowing us to dissociate spatially driven and reward-driven remapping. VR provided tight control over sensory stimuli and the animal’s trajectory, allowing us to observe neuronal remapping in relation to distant rewards, a phenomenon potentially less visible in freely moving scenarios. Furthermore, the task included multiple updates to a hidden reward zone across two environments, allowing us to dissociate spatially driven and reward-driven remapping. Imaging began on day 1 of task acquisition, in which animals traversed a unidirectional 450 cm virtual linear track (for example, Environment 1 (ENV 1)) with a hidden 50 cm reward zone where sucrose water was delivered operantly for licking (Fig. 1d,e). On day 3, the reward zone was moved within a ‘switch’ session to a new track location, signaled by automatic reward delivery on the first ten post-switch trials only if the mouse failed to lick in the new zone. On day 5, the reward zone was moved to a third location, then returned to its original location on day 7. On day 8, the reward switch coincided with the introduction of a novel environment (for example, ENV 2), in which the switch order was then reversed (Fig. 1e). On ‘stay’ days, the last reward location from the previous day was maintained. A separate ‘fixed-condition’ group of mice (n = 3) experienced only one reward location in a single environment (ENV 1) (Extended Data Fig. 1), to control for the effect of experience. To maintain engagement, the reward was randomly omitted on ~15% of trials. At the end of the track, mice passed through a variable length gray ‘teleport’ zone (~50 cm + temporal jitter up to 10 s; Methods) before starting the next lap.\n     4→\n     5→# Behavioral training and VR tasks\n     6→\n     7→## Pre-training\n     8→\n     9→Mice were pre-trained on a ‘running’ task on a 350 cm virtual linear track with random black and white checkerboard walls and a white floor to collect a cued reward. The reward location was marked by two large gray towers, initially positioned 50 cm down the track. The mouse was rewarded for licking within 25 cm of the towers, otherwise, an automatic reward was given at the towers. The towers then disappeared and quickly reappeared further down the track. Covering the distance to the current reward in under 20 s increased the inter-reward distance by 10 cm, but if the mouse took longer than 30 s, the distance decreased by 10 cm, with a maximum reward location of 300 cm. Upon consistent completion of 300 cm distance in under 20 s, the automatic reward was turned off, requiring the mice to lick to receive reward. At the end of the track, the mice passed through a black curtain and into a gray ‘teleport zone’ (50 cm long) that was equiluminant with the VR envir\n...\n\n=== Step 36 methods.txt ===\n100→To quantify the degree of backward and forward shifting of place fields following a reward zone switch, we first identified the formation lap (trial) of each field post-switch by adapting a previously established procedure31. Within the bounds of each trial-averaged field ±10 cm after the switch, we identified trials in which the post-switch spatially binned activity exceeded its mean +1 s.d. We then found the first five-trial window with at least three trials above this threshold and took the first active trial in that window as the formation lap31. To compute the field’s center of mass on each lap with maximum spatial resolution, we used the raw deconvolved activity and position within the bounds of the trial-averaged field. We then subtracted the center of mass on the formation lap from the center of mass of the field’s mean activity in the last 30 trials of the session to quantify the degree of shift (negative values indicate a backwards shift).\n   101→\n   102→# Decoding of RR position\n   103→\n   104→We implemented a previously developed decoder that uses circular–linear regression38 to predict the animal’s position in circular, RR coordinates from the deconvolved calcium event timeseries (at speeds of >2 cm s−1) of RR, TR or non-RR remapping neurons. The model ‘decode score’ at each timepoint t is given by \\cos ({{y}_{t}}-\\hat{{y}}_{t}), where is the animal’s true RR position and \\hat{{y}}_{t} is the predicted RR position. A score of one indicates perfect prediction and zero indicates random prediction. We performed tenfold cross-validation in which we trained the decoder on 90% of the data and tested it on a held-out 10%, either training and testing within the trials before the switch or training on one set (before) and testing on the other set (after). Figure 3a shows decoder performance from a single cross-validation fold, considering all data from an example session. To quantify decoding accuracy compared to chance, we next downsampled the data to match occupancy at each RR position bin (bin size 2π / 45, ~10 cm), either within trials before the switch or in the last 30 trials after the switch (thereby matching trial numbers before and after). We then repeated model training and testing with 100 shuffled datasets for each session and cell subpopulation. In each shuffle, the timeseries of each cell was independently circularly shifted by a uniform random amount up to the number of samples in the session minus one. In Fig. 3b, model performance is reported as a mean decode score over time samples and cross-validation folds compared to the mean of the shuffles for each session. To evaluate decoder performance across sessions, we z-scored the decoder score of each session to its shuffle and binned the z-score as a function of RR position, then averaged across animals (Fig. 3c).\n   105→\n   106→# Sequence detection and quantification\n   107→\n   108→For analysis of behavioral timescale sequential firing of neural subpopulations, we used the\n...","stderr":"","interrupted":false,"isImage":false,"noOutputExpected":false}}

Both environments consisted of a 450 cm linear track, with two colored towers (green in ENV 1, blue in ENV 2) and two patterned towers along the walls. Environments were further distinguished by the frequency of diagonal wall gratings (low, ENV 1; high, ENV 2) and color of the sky (dark gray, ENV 1; light gray, ENV 2). The reward zone was a ‘hidden’, unmarked 50 cm span at one of three possible locations along the track, each equidistantly spaced between the towers to control for proximity of each reward to visual landmarks: zone A, 80–130 cm; zone B, 200–250 cm; zone C, 320–370 cm. Only one reward zone was ever active at a time. Reward was randomly omitted on approximately 15% of trials, determined by a random number generator for each trial. Each lap terminated in a black curtain following by a ‘teleport period’ that began with an unchanging gray display for a randomly jittered amount of time (‘jitter period’) between 1 and 5 s (5–10 s on trials following reward omissions or trials in which the mouse did not lick in the reward zone), after which the gray display began to move again for 50 cm giving the appearance of a gray ‘tunnel,’ before the mouse re-entered the virtual environment at 0 cm.\n13:     14→\n14:     15→Each mouse encountered a different starting reward zone and sequence of reward zone switches, counterbalanced across mice (n = 11 mice). Most mice began the task in ENV 1 (n = 9 mice; two mice (m17 and m18) began in ENV 2). An initial reward zone (for example, A as in Fig. 1e) was acquired for days 1–2 of the task. On day 3 (switch one), the zone was moved after 30 trials to one of the two other possible locations on the track (for example, B). On the first ten trials of any new condition, including the first day and each subsequent switch, the reward was automatically delivered at the end of the zone if the mouse had not yet licked within the zone, to signal reward availability. Otherwise, the reward was delivered at the first location within the zone where the mouse licked. After these ten trials, a reward was operantly delivered for licking within the zone, and we observed that the mice often started licking before ten trials elapsed (Extended Data Fig. 1a–c). The new reward zone was maintained on day 4. On day 5 (switch two), the zone was moved to the third possible reward zone (for example, C), maintained on day 6 and moved back to the original location on day 7 (for example, A). On day 8, the reward zone switch coincided with a switch into the novel environment, where the sequence of zone switches was then reversed on the same day-to-day schedule for a total of 14 days (Fig. 1 and Extended Data Fig. 1). The session was terminated early if the mouse ceased licking or running consistently and/or if the imaging time exceeded 50 min. Before the imaging session, mice were provided 30 ‘warm-up’ trials using the task and reward zone from the previous day to re-acclimate them to the VR setup (using the pre-training environment on day 1 of imaging). Following each daily imaging session, mice were given another ~100 training trials without imaging on the last reward zone seen in the imaging session until they acquired their daily water allotment.\n19:     20→\n20:     21→# 2P imaging\n---\n19:     20→\n20:     21→# 2P imaging\n21:     22→\n22:     23→We used a resonant-galvo scanning 2P microscope from Neurolabware with Scanbox software (https://scanbox.org, v.4.7) operated through MATLAB (Mathworks) to image the calcium activity of CA1 neurons. Excitation was achieved using 920 nm light from a tunable output femtosecond pulsed laser (Coherent Chameleon Discovery). Laser power modulation was accomplished with a Pockels cell (Conoptics). The typical excitation power, measured at the front face of the objective (Leica ×25, 1.0 NA, 2.6 mm working distance), ranged from 15–68 mW for mice m2–m7, 50–100 mW for m10 and m11, and 13–40 mW for m12–19. The task was imaged starting from day 1 for all mice except m11, for whom imaging started on day 3 owing to lower viral expression. For most animals and sessions, to minimize photodamage and photobleaching, the Pockels cell was used to reduce laser power to minimal levels between trials (during the teleport period), except for mice m11–m14 on task days 1, 7, 8 and 14, and mice m15–m19 on day 1 and all switch days, for which laser power was maintained and imaging continued throughout the teleport period. Photons were collected using Hamamatsu gated GAsP photomultiplier tubes (part H11706-401 for Neurolabware Microscopes). The imaging field of view (FOV) was collected at 1.0 magnification with unidirectional scanning at ~15.5 Hz, resulting in an approximately 0.7 × 0.7 mm FOV (512 × 796 pixels). An electrically tunable lens was used to simultaneously image deep and superficial CA1 in two mice (m17 and m18), in two planes separated by ~27 µm, with frames bidirectionally imaged at ~31 Hz interleaved in the scan for a sampling rate of ~15.5 Hz per plane. In all mice, the same FOV was acquired each session by aligning to a reference image from previous days before the start of data acquisition, with the aid of the ‘searchref’ plugin in Scanbox. This allowed us to track single cells across days.\n---\n21:     22→\n22:     23→We used a resonant-galvo scanning 2P microscope from Neurolabware with Scanbox software (https://scanbox.org, v.4.7) operated through MATLAB (Mathworks) to image the calcium activity of CA1 neurons. This allowed us to track single cells across days.\n23:     24→\n24:     25→# Calcium data processing\n---\n23:     24→\n24:     25→# Calcium data processing\n25:     26→\n26:     27→The Suite2P software package (v.0.10.3) was used to perform xy motion correction (rigid and non-rigid) and identify putative cell regions of interest (ROIs). Manual curation eliminated ROIs containing multiple somata or dendrites, lacking visually obvious transients, suspected of overexpressing the calcium indicator or exhibiting high and continuous fluorescence fluctuation typical of putative interneurons. This approach yielded 155–2172 putative pyramidal neurons per session, owing to variation in imaging window implant quality and viral expression. In multi-plane imaged animals (see ‘2P imaging’), ROIs were identified separately per plane, but planes were pooled for all analyses except those in Extended Data Fig. 7. Custom code was used to follow individual ROIs across imaging sessions using the intersection over union of their pixels. The threshold for ROI matching was chosen algorithmically for each dataset such that the intersection over union for the best match for an ROI pair was always greater than the intersection over union for any second-best match.\n---\n25:     26→\n26:     27→The Suite2P software package (v.0.10.3) was used to perform xy motion correction (rigid and non-rigid) and identify putative cell regions of interest (ROIs). This approach yielded 155–2172 putative pyramidal neurons per session, owing to variation in imaging window implant quality and viral expression. In multi-plane imaged animals (see ‘2P imaging’), ROIs were identified separately per plane, but planes were pooled for all analyses except those in Extended Data Fig. 7. Custom code was used to follow individual ROIs across imaging sessions using the intersection over union of their pixels. The threshold for ROI matching was chosen algorithmically for each dataset such that the intersection over union for the best match for an ROI pair was always greater than the intersection over union for any second-best match.\n27:     28→\n28:     29→To compute the ΔF/F (dF/F) for each ROI, baseline fluorescence was calculated within each trial independently using a maximin procedure with a 20 s sliding window. Limitation to individual trials both accounts for potential photobleaching over the session and avoids the teleport periods for sessions during which the laser power was reduced (see ‘2P imaging’). The value of dF/F was then calculated for each cell as the fluorescence minus the baseline, divided by the absolute value of the baseline, then smoothed with a two-sample (~0.129 s) s.d. Gaussian kernel. The activity rate was extracted by deconvolving dF/F with a canonical calcium kernel using the OASIS algorithm as used in Suite2p. We note that this deconvolution is not interpreted as a spike rate but rather as a method to eliminate the asymmetric smoothing of the calcium signal induced by indicator kinetics. Additional putative interneurons were detected for exclusion from further analysis by a Pearson correlation of >0.5 between their dF/F timeseries and the animal’s running speed, excluding 0.42 ± 0.85% of cells (mean ± s.d. across mice and days).\n---\n27:     28→\n28:     29→To compute the ΔF/F (dF/F) for each ROI, baseline fluorescence was calculated within each trial independently using a maximin procedure with a 20 s sliding window. Limitation to individual trials both accounts for potential photobleaching over the session and avoids the teleport periods for sessions during which the laser power was reduced (see ‘2P imaging’). The value of dF/F was then calculated for each cell as the fluorescence minus the baseline, divided by the absolute value of the baseline, then smoothed with a two-sample (~0.129 s) s.d. Gaussian kernel. The activity rate was extracted by deconvolving dF/F with a canonical calcium kernel using the OASIS algorithm as used in Suite2p. We note that this deconvolution is not interpreted as a spike rate but rather as a method to eliminate the asymmetric smoothing of the calcium signal induced by indicator kinetics. Additional putative interneurons were detected for exclusion from further analysis by a Pearson correlation of >0.5 between their dF/F timeseries and the animal’s running speed, excluding 0.42 ± 0.85% of cells (mean ± s.d. across mice and days).\n29:     30→\n30:     31→# Statistics and reproducibility\n---\n39:     40→\n40:     41→AP and ML ROI coordinates were computed as the 2D center of mass of ROI pixels, relative to each FOV. In multi-plane imaged animals, ROIs were separated according to their deep or superficial imaging plane. To estimate deep-superficial (dorso-ventral (DV)) coordinates in single-plane animals, we acquired a z-stack scan after the day 14 task session that extended −100 µm below (−60 µm below in one mouse) to +100 µm above the FOV in the DV axis, in 2 µm steps with ~50 frames per step. Each step was independently motion-corrected as described above, and the mean of each step was used to compile the 3D z-stack array. We then took the AP projection of each ML slice of the z-stack (that is, looking at the side view of the z-stack). We minimum-filtered (4 × 0 pixel window, ML × DV) and smoothed (2 × 2 pixel s.d. Gaussian) each slice, then detected the most prominent dip in brightness in the DV axis to approximate the location of cell bodies, under the assumption that the GCaMP signal is typically absent in the nuclei and brighter in the surrounding neuropil. This yielded a per-pixel estimate of the depth of the pyramidal layer center from the dorsal surface of the z-stack, which we then smoothed (40 × 40 pixel s.d. Gaussian) to approximate the cell layer ‘curvature’ across the FOV. The curvature estimate was registered to the mean FOV of each imaging session. Given that the imaging plane transects this curvature, calculating the mean DV distance of each ROI (across pixels) from the curvature provides an estimate of where each ROI resides in the deep-to-superficial axis.\n41:     42→\n42:     43→# Quantification of licking behavior\n---\n41:     42→\n42:     43→# Quantification of licking behavior\n43:     44→\n44:     45→The capacitive lick sensor allowed us to detect single licks. A very small number of trials with erroneous lick detection from damage to the circuit were removed from subsequent licking analysis by setting these values to NaN (~0.65% of all imaged trials, n = 81 out of 12,376 trials removed across 11 switch mice). These trials were detected by >30% of the 0.0645 s imaging frame samples in the trial containing a cumulative lick count >2, as this would have produced a sustained lick rate ≥20 Hz. Remaining lick counts were converted to a binary vector and spatially binned at the same resolution as neural activity (10 cm bins), then divided by the time occupancy in each bin to yield a lick rate. The reward zone itself is thus excluded to exclude consummatory licks (Extended Data Fig. 1e). A ratio of 1 indicates perfect licking only in the anticipatory zone, a ratio of −1 indicates licking only outside of this zone and a ratio of 0 indicates chance licking everywhere, excluding the reward zone.\n---\n47:     48→\n48:     49→where Lickin is the mean lick rate in a 50 cm ‘anticipatory’ zone before the start of the reward zone and Lickout is the mean rate outside of this zone and the reward zone. To obtain an activity matrix of trials × position bins for each cell, we binned the 450 cm linear track into 45 bins of 10 cm each. We took the mean dF/F or deconvolved calcium activity on each trial within each position bin (note that taking the mean activity over time samples is equivalent to normalizing by the occupancy within a trial).\n53:     54→\n54:     55→We defined place cells as cells with significant SI65 in either trial set 1 (pre-reward-switch) or trial set 2 (post-reward-switch) in a session. On days without a switch, we used the first and second halves of trials. For the SI, we used the deconvolved calcium activity as this reduces the asymmetry of the calcium signal. On days without a switch, we used the first and second halves of trials. For the SI, we used the deconvolved calcium activity as this reduces the asymmetry of the calcium signal. SI was calculated for calcium imaging as:\n55:     56→\n56:     57→{{\\rm{SI}}}=\\mathop{\\sum }\\limits_{i=1}^{N}{p}_{i}\\frac{{f}_{i}}{f}{\\rm{log}}_{2}\\frac{{f}_{i}}{f},\n---\n57:     58→\n58:     59→where pi is the occupancy probability in position bin i for the whole session, fi is the trial-averaged activity per position bin i and f is the mean activity over the whole session, computed as the sum of fi × pi over all N bins. To determine pi, we calculated the per-trial occupancy (number of imaging samples) in each bin divided by the total number of samples per trial, then summed the occupancy probabilities across trials and divided by the total per session. To determine the significance of the SI scores, we created a null distribution by circularly permuting the position data relative to the timeseries of each cell, by a random amount between ~1 s and the length of the trial, independently on each trial. SI was calculated from the trial-averaged activity of each shuffle, and this shuffle procedure was repeated 100 times per cell. A cell’s true SI was considered significant if it exceeded 95% of the SI scores from all shuffles within an animal, pooled across cells (more stringent than comparing to the shuffle of each individual cell). For plotting place cell firing over trials (for example, Fig. 2), deconvolved calcium activity was normalized to the mean of each cell within a session and smoothed with a 10 cm s.d. Gaussian.\n59:     60→\n60:     61→# Spatial peak firing identification\n---\n65:     66→\n66:     67→Correlation matrices were computed using the spatially binned deconvolved activity on each trial, smoothed with a 20 cm s.d. Gaussian for single cells and a 10 cm s.d. Gaussian for population vectors. This resulted in a matrix, A, of j trials × m position bins per cell. For population vectors, single-cell matrices were horizontally concatenated such that A was j trials × m position bins × n cells. Each trial was z-scored across the position axis. We then computed the trial-wise spatial peak error for each RR cell as the difference between the cell’s spatial peak location on each trial and its trial-averaged spatial peak location within either the trials before or after the reward switch (circular error converted back to cm). We next performed a Spearman correlation between the distance run during the teleport and the error on the following trial (Extended Data Fig. 2e,f).\n87:     88→\n88:     89→# Individual place field analysis\n---\n91:     92→\n92:     93→Using the spatially binned, deconvolved activity smoothed with a 10 cm s.d. Gaussian, excluding activity from movement speeds <2 cm s−1, candidate place fields were identified as regions of ≥20 cm that exceeded 20% of the maximum of the trial-averaged activity19 (over the 30 pre-switch trials or the final 30 post-switch trials). To include a given field, the cell was required to be significantly active within the field boundaries in at least eight (>25%) of the 30 trials, where ‘significantly active’ was defined as the raw, un-binned deconvolved activity at those positions exceeding its mean +1 s.d. across the 30 trials. For analyses of place field properties before versus after a reward switch, cells were excluded that did not have significant SI or a significant field both before and after the switch. The fraction of cell remapping types meeting these criteria on specific days is reported in Extended Data Fig. 3c. For analyses of place field width and in-field activity rate, cells were further excluded if one of their fields overlapped the first or last spatial bin of the track, as this prevented accurate detection of field boundaries that extended into the teleport zone (which was not imaged for some animals; see ‘2P imaging’). Place field width was defined as the last minus the first position bin that crossed the initial 20% activity threshold.\n93:     94→\n94:     95→## Coordination of remapping between fields\n---\n98:     99→\n99:    100→To quantify the degree of backward and forward shifting of place fields following a reward zone switch, we first identified the formation lap (trial) of each field post-switch by adapting a previously established procedure31. Within the bounds of each trial-averaged field ±10 cm after the switch, we identified trials in which the post-switch spatially binned activity exceeded its mean +1 s.d. We then found the first five-trial window with at least three trials above this threshold and took the first active trial in that window as the formation lap31. To compute the field’s center of mass on each lap with maximum spatial resolution, we used the raw deconvolved activity and position within the bounds of the trial-averaged field. VR provided tight control over sensory stimuli and the animal’s trajectory, allowing us to observe neuronal remapping in relation to distant rewards, a phenomenon potentially less visible in freely moving scenarios. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01ENEZsJhBgwR6NYQuB8EMLv","type":"tool_result","content":"2P imaging (described below), with one imaging session per day (mean ± s.d., 80.5 ± 7.4 trials across 14 mice, all imaging days). The session was terminated early if the mouse ceased licking or running consistently and/or if the imaging time exceeded 50 min. Before the imaging session, mice were provided 30 ‘warm-up’ trials using the task and reward zone from the previous day to re-acclimate them to the VR setup (using the pre-training environment on day 1 of imaging). Following each daily imaging session, mice were given another ~100 training trials without imaging on the last reward zone seen in the imaging session until they acquired their daily water allotment.\n    20→\n    21→# 2P imaging\n    22→\n    23→We used a resonant-galvo scanning 2P microscope from Neurolabware with Scanbox software (https://scanbox.org, v.4.7) operated through MATLAB (Mathworks) to image the calcium activity of CA1 neurons. Excitation was achieved using 920 nm light from a tunable output femtosecond pulsed laser (Coherent Chameleon Discovery). Laser power modulation was accomplished with a Pockels cell (Conoptics). The typical excitation power, measured at the front face of the objective (Leica ×25, 1.0 NA, 2.6 mm working distance), ranged from 15–68 mW for mice m2–m7, 50–100 mW for m10 and m11, and 13–40 mW for m12–19. The task was imaged starting from day 1 for all mice except m11, for whom imaging started on day 3 owing to lower viral expression. For most animals and sessions, to minimize photodamage and photobleaching, the Pockels cell was used to reduce laser power to minimal levels between trials (during the teleport period), except for mice m11–m14 on task days 1, 7, 8 and 14, and mice m15–m19 on day 1 and all switch days, for which laser power was maintained and imaging continued throughout the teleport period. Photons were collected using Hamamatsu gated GAsP photomultiplier tubes (part H11706-401 for Neurolabware Microscopes). The imaging field of view (FOV) was collected at 1.0 magnification with unidirectional scanning at ~15.5 Hz, resulting in an approximately 0.7 × 0.7 mm FOV (512 × 796 pixels). An electrically tunable lens was used to simultaneously image deep and superficial CA1 in two mice (m17 and m18), in two planes separated by ~27 µm, with frames bidirectionally imaged at ~31 Hz interleaved in the scan for a sampling rate of ~15.5 Hz per plane. In all mice, the same FOV was acquired each session by aligning to a reference image from previous days before the start of data acquisition, with the aid of the ‘searchref’ plugin in Scanbox. This allowed us to track single cells across days.\n    24→\n    25→# Calcium data processing\n    26→\n    27→The Suite2P software package (v.0.10.3) was used to perform xy motion correction (rigid and non-rigid) and identify putative cell regions of interest (ROIs). Manual curation eliminated ROIs containing multiple somata or dendrites, lacking visually obvious transients, suspected of overexpressing the calcium indicator or exhibiting high and continuous fluorescence fluctuation typical of putative interneurons. This approach yielded 155–2172 putative pyramidal neurons per session, owing to variation in imaging window implant quality and viral expression. In multi-plane imaged animals (see ‘2P imaging’), ROIs were identified separately per plane, but planes were pooled for all analyses except those in Extended Data Fig. 7. The typical excitation power, measured at the front face of the objective (Leica ×25, 1.0 NA, 2.6 mm working distance), ranged from 15–68 mW for mice m2–m7, 50–100 mW for m10 and m11, and 13–40 mW for m12–19. Manual curation eliminated ROIs containing multiple somata or dendrites, lacking visually obvious transients, suspected of overexpressing the calcium indicator or exhibiting high and continuous fluorescence fluctuation typical of putative interneurons. This approach yielded 155–2172 putative pyramidal neurons per session, owing to variation in imaging window implant quality and viral expression. In multi-plane imaged animals (see ‘2P imaging’), ROIs were identified separately per plane, but planes were pooled for all analyses except those in Extended Data Fig. 7. Custom code was used to follow individual ROIs across imaging sessions using the intersection over union of their pixels. The threshold for ROI matching was chosen algorithmically for each dataset such that the intersection over union for the best match for an ROI pair was always greater than the intersection over union for any second-best match.\n    28→\n    29→To compute the ΔF/F (dF/F) for each ROI, baseline fluorescence was calculated within each trial independently using a maximin procedure with a 20 s sliding window. Limitation to individual trials both accounts for potential photobleaching over the session and avoids the teleport periods for sessions during which the laser power was reduced (see ‘2P imaging’). The value of dF/F was then calculated for each cell as the fluorescence minus the baseline, divided by the absolute value of the baseline, then smoothed with a two-sample (~0.129 s) s.d. Gaussian kernel. The activity rate was extracted by deconvolving dF/F with a canonical calcium kernel using the OASIS algorithm as used in Suite2p. We note that this deconvolution is not interpreted as a spike rate but rather as a method to eliminate the asymmetric smoothing of the calcium signal induced by indicator kinetics. Additional putative interneurons were detected for exclusion from further analysis by a Pearson correlation of >0.5 between their dF/F timeseries and the animal’s running speed, excluding 0.42 ± 0.85% of cells (mean ± s.d. across mice and days).\n    30→\n    31→# Statistics and reproducibility\n    32→\n    33→To avoid assumptions about data distributions, we used nonparametric tests or permutation tests in most cases, except when a Shapiro–Wilk test for normality confirmed that parametric tests were reasonable. Let me also check one more thing about the lick indexing issue in the reference code:\n\nIn the reference code `get_timeseries_data`:\n- Line 112: `if sum(licks[start:stop] > 2)/len(licks[start-1:stop-1]) > 0.35:`\n  - Note: `licks[start:stop]` uses 1-indexed start/stop BUT `licks[start-1:stop-1]` uses 0-indexed\n  - This is a potential bug in the reference code or different indexing convention\n- The reference code applies `start-1:stop-1` for trial data because indices are 1-indexed, but the lick error check mixes `start:stop` in the numerator with `start-1:stop-1` in the denominator, which seems inconsistent.\n\nLooking at the `get_reward_zones` function, the agent couldn't directly use it since it requires session metadata. The key patterns I'm seeing across the agent's decisions are: data loading through h5py for NWB files organized by subject subdirectories, subject splitting by directory name, session splitting where each NWB file represents one session, and trial splitting based on the data structure.\n\nFor trial filtering, sessions with fewer than 2 trials are excluded and trials with less than 5 timepoints are skipped. The neural data comes from deconvolved calcium events, processed through iscell filtering and interneuron removal based on dF/F-speed correlation thresholds above 0.5, with NaN values replaced by zeros. Temporal alignment anchors to trial start at the first frame after teleport, using a fixed frame period of approximately 64.5 milliseconds rather than per-session calibration.\n\nEnvironment type comes from the NWB file's environment variable, trial numbering uses the loop index rather than the NWB trial number field, and previous trial outcomes are extracted from reward event timestamps. Position is binned into 5 equal 90-centimeter segments, distance to reward zone is discretized into 7 bins based on signed distance to the nearest edge, and lick data is binarized with error correction for samples exceeding 2.
